    Important Words
    • vraja - striyaḥ — the women of Vraja
      Compound: vraja + strī (woman). The gopīs again — the Mathurā women have shifted from generic praise (gopyaḥ in 14) to the more intimate ranking: vraja-striyaḥ are the most fortunate of all women, dhanyāḥ.
    • anurakta - dhiyaḥ — with attached minds
      Compound: anurakta (attached, dyed-after) + dhī (intelligence). The very technical Gauḍīya word — anurāga, the deep dye of attachment, is rāgānuga-bhakti's signature. The Mathurā women describe exactly the bhāva they themselves are aspiring to.
    • aśru - kaṇṭhyaḥ — with throats choked with tears
      Compound: aśru (tear) + kaṇṭha (throat). One of the eight sāttvika-bhāvas (svara-bheda) enumerated by Rūpa Gosvāmī in Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu. The Mathurā women diagnose the gopīs as exhibiting the highest bodily signs of prema.
    • urukrama - citta - yānāḥ — whose minds have gone to Urukrama
      Compound: urukrama (the wide-strider, Viṣṇu) + citta + yāna (going). Their cittas have left their bodies and gone to the Lord. Cf. SB 1.5.22 — Vyāsa's instruction to Nārada: glorify only Urukrama.
    • mathana — churning
      From √math. The Yaśodā verb (SB 10.9). The Mathurā women, listing the gopīs' daily work, name mathana first among the activities woven with kṛṣṇa-kīrtana — quoting, perhaps unknowingly, Vraja's most famous scene.
    The ladies of Vraja are the most fortunate of women because, with their minds fully attached to Kṛṣṇa and their throats always choked up with tears, they constantly sing about Him while milking the cows, winnowing grain, churning butter, gathering cow dung for fuel, riding on swings, taking care of their crying babies, sprinkling the ground with water, cleaning their houses, and so on. By their exalted Kṛṣṇa consciousness they automatically acquire all desirable things.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The ladies of Vraja are the most fortunate of women because, with their minds fully attached to Kṛṣṇa and their throats always choked up with tears, they constantly sing about Him while milking the cows, winnowing grain, churning butter, gathering cow dung for fuel, riding on swings, taking care of their crying babies, sprinkling the ground with water, cleaning their houses, and so on. By their exalted Kṛṣṇa consciousness they automatically acquire all desirable things.
    Commentary

    "And even while doing household chores, the gopis were not stopped from drinking the nectar of his sweetness." That is the intent of this verse. "While milking the cows and doing other chores, such as swinging or sprinkling water on the ground (uksana), they would sing about his name, qualities and form and (ca) see him. In this way, with their tongues they tasted and with their eyes they tasted, without interruption. Krsna, being completely attracted to them (anurakta dhiyah), carries them in his heart (urukrama citta yanah) (though brahma and others carry him in their hearts)." In this way the great fortune of the gopis is expressed.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Even when the gopīs could not directly see Kṛṣṇa, he manifested to them as if he was present. While milking cows or threshing grains, they sometimes saw him (ca) and sometimes sang about him. When they did not see that form, that form (enam) appeared before them as a sphūrti. Because we do not sing like that, we are most unfortunate. Out of great attraction their throats became filled with tears. Or, because they were attached to him, they became overcome with crying (aśru-kaṇṭyaḥ). He made his appearance in their hearts, wherever they went (urukrama -citta-yānāḥ). Another version has cintayānāḥ (their thoughts went to him).

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Even when the gopīs could not directly see Kṛṣṇa, he manifested to them as if he was present. While milking cows or threshing grains, they sometimes saw him (ca) and sometimes sang about him. When they did not see that form, that form (enam) appeared before them as a sphūrti. Because we do not sing like that, we are most unfortunate. Out of great attraction their throats became filled with tears. Or, because they were attached to him, they became overcome with crying (aśru-kaṇṭyaḥ). He made his appearance in their hearts, wherever they went (urukrama –citta-yānāḥ). Another version has cintayānāḥ (their thoughts went to him).

    Related values & verses

    Anahankarah (Freedom from False Ego)Karuna (Kindness / Compassion)Tyaga (Renunciation / Letting Go)
    Related verses
    BG 15.9 BG 18.64 BG 2.62 BG 3.11 BG 12.3 SB 4.29.2 BG 9.11 BG 7.8 BG 10.3 BG 13.1 BG 5.18 BG 6.1 BG 6.5 BG 1.1 BG 16.4 BG 1.41
    Related lectures
    • Radha Raman Sw SB 10-44-15-18 - 2023-08-04 ISKCON Vrindavan
      His Holiness Radha Raman Swami· 2023-08-04· English
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    The gopīs sing of Him while milking, winnowing, churning, gathering cow-dung, swinging, soothing crying babies, sprinkling the courtyard, cleaning the house. The work goes on; the song does not stop. Their life is the perfected form of what Yaśodā's churning verse already showed — bhakti braided into the day's rope.

    Stories
    Yaśodā's churning song (SB 10.9)

    In Canto 10's ninth chapter, mother Yaśodā churns yogurt while singing Kṛṣṇa's pastimes — the rope of the churn and the rope of remembrance pulled together. The Mathurā women in this verse are describing the same Vraja, only widened: every gopī, in every house, performing the same braid Yaśodā performs in one.

    Gauḍīyas read this verse as the seed of the Hare Kṛṣṇa mahāmantra's practical use: chanting and household work are not opposed; they are made for each other. The hand churns, the voice sings, the dhī attaches itself anyway.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.16

    ↗
    ప్రాతర్వ్రజాద్ వ్రజత ఆవిశతశ్చ సాయంగోభి: సమం క్‍వణయతోఽస్య నిశమ్య వేణుమ్ । నిర్గమ్య తూర్ణమబలా: పథి భూరిపుణ్యా:పశ్యన్తి సస్మితముఖం సదయావలోకమ్ ॥ ౧౬ ॥

    Important Words
    • bhūri - puṇyāḥ — of abundant piety
      Compound: bhūri (much) + puṇya (merit). The Bhāgavatam uses this phrase for those whose past sukṛti has ripened into the chance for darśana. Gauḍīyas read it as bhakti-unmukhī-sukṛti — the merit that turns the face toward Bhagavān.
    • sa - smita - mukham — with smiling face
      Compound: sa (with) + smita (smile) + mukha (face). His face on the road carries the same smile as His face in the arena (verse 12). The Bhāgavatam keeps the smile constant across rasas — vātsalya, sakhya, mādhurya all see it.
    • sa - daya - avalokam — with merciful glance
      Compound: sa + daya (mercy) + avaloka (glance). The kaṭākṣa — the side-glance — is in Gauḍīya theology the channel by which Bhagavān transmits anugraha. The gopīs are receiving kaṭākṣa-vidyā on a village road.
    • veṇum — flute
      Accusative of veṇu. The instrument that the Mathurā women bring up twice (cf. 13). They will never hear it themselves; but they have already heard about it. Bhakti often begins as hearing-about.
    When the gopīs hear Kṛṣṇa playing His flute as He leaves Vraja in the morning with His cows or returns with them at sunset, the young girls quickly come out of their houses to see Him. They must have performed many pious activities to be able to see Him as He walks on the road, His smiling face mercifully glancing upon them.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    When the gopīs hear Kṛṣṇa playing His flute as He leaves Vraja in the morning with His cows or returns with them at sunset, the young girls quickly come out of their houses to see Him. They must have performed many pious activities to be able to see Him as He walks on the road, His smiling face mercifully glancing upon them.
    Commentary

    "When Krsna goes to the forerst from Vraja in the morning, and when he returns from the forest in the evening, they hear his flute and come out of their houses. They see him in the small groves situated on the cow paths. Seeing that they are suffering due to separation, he glances on them mercifully to fulfill their desires. This is the fortune of the women of Vraja."

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This verse describes how they drink his beauty with their eyes. In the morning he goes with the cows and the cowherd boys while playing his flute. Understanding his presence by hearing the flute (niśamya), they quickly come out of their houses. Mention of the flute also suggests the beauty of his face. tat tvan-mukhaṁ katham ivāmbuja-tulya-kakṣaṁ vācām avāci nanu parvaṇi parvaṇīndoḥ | tat kiṁ bruve kim aparaṁ bhuvanaika-kānta- veṇu tvad-ānanam anena samaṁ nu yat syāt || In describing your face, how can it be compared to a lotus? Since the moon withers with each phase, words of comparison with your face also wither. Do you speak of comparing your face to another face? How can I say that your face is equal to another face? Kṛṣṇa-karṇāmṛta 97 The gopīs become so weak (abalāḥ) that they are unable to milk the cows because of hearing the flute and their continuously intense prema. Thus they stumble and fall here and there. Or they are so weak that they cannot go to meet him, and thus they simply look at him on the road. He becomes even more beautiful with his smile and his merciful glance. He thus enters Vraja in the most remarkable way (ā—viśataḥ), revealing his excellent forest dress, with the cows and cowherd boys.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Part This verse describes how they drink his beauty with their eyes. In the morning he goes with the cows and the cowherd boys while playing his flute.     Concepts
    Punya
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Morning and evening, Kṛṣṇa walks the road of Vraja with the cows, the flute moving the air. The gopīs come out of their houses just to be seen by Him — like sunflowers turning when the curtain is pulled. Darśana is not their reward; it is their faculty.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.17

    ↗
    ఏవం ప్రభాషమాణాసు స్త్రీషు యోగేశ్వరో హరి: । శత్రుం హన్తుం మనశ్చక్రే భగవాన్ భరతర్షభ ॥ ౧౭ ॥
    evaṁ prabhāṣamāṇāsu

    O hero of the Bhāratas .
    Important Words
    • yoga - īśvaraḥ — master of mystic power
      Compound: yoga + īśvara (lord). The same epithet from BG 18.78 (yatra yogeśvaraḥ kṛṣṇaḥ). The Gauḍīya doctrine: all yoga-śakti is His property; nothing is achievable without His sanction.
    • hantum — to kill
      Infinitive of √han. The same verb used in BG 1.34 (Arjuna refusing to kill) — here, the Lord Himself decides to kill. The reversal is doctrinal: killing demons is His prerogative, never the bhakta's by personal will.
    • manaḥ cakre — He made up His mind
      Idiomatic — 'made the mind'. The Bhāgavatam uses this phrase for the moment Bhagavān's līlā-saṅkalpa crystallizes into action. From this verb, every subsequent event of the chapter follows.
    • bharata - ṛṣabha — O bull of the Bhāratas (Parīkṣit)
      Compound: bharata + ṛṣabha (bull, chief). Śuka addresses Parīkṣit at the chapter's hinge — a Bhāgavata signature: the most violent līlā is told to the most peace-loving king, just before he himself faces death.
    [Śukadeva Gosvāmī continued:] As the women spoke thus, O hero of the Bhāratas, Lord Kṛṣṇa, the master of all mystic power, made up His mind to kill His opponent.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    [Śukadeva Gosvāmī continued:] As the women spoke thus, O hero of the Bhāratas, Lord Kṛṣṇa, the master of all mystic power, made up His mind to kill His opponent.
    Commentary

    Krsna was the best of yogis. Though the people seated around the arena were talking to themselves, Krsna could hear it, being the best of yogis. Hearing their talk, he decided to kill Canura, as what was the necessity of giving those attracted women suffering.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Even though they could see him at other times, the gopīs were able to do this without restriction in front of others on the pretext of his leaving and entering Vraja (along with all others). The women of Mathurā speak like this since it is very possible that Kṛṣṇa will return there to remove their feelings of separation. We are most unfortunate since we cannot see him as described. The women spoke in this manner with fear, out of pain caused by prema (pra—bhāsamānāṣu), and could not stop speaking in this manner. The locative case indicates that this speech was befitting. Arhānarhayoḥ...???? Kṛṣṇa was able to reveal or hide his powers at will (yogeśvaraḥ). He also wanted to remove the burden of the earth (hariḥ). Thus it was natural that he desired to kill his opponent—an enemy with actual animosity (śatrum). And he was Bhagavān. This was his ultimate identity, and the final cause of his desire to kill the enemy. O best of the Bhāratas! Addressing Parīkṣit, Śukadeva gives him joy since he was discontent at the delay in killing Cāṇūra in the wrestling match. Since you come in the dynasty of Bharata, you know such pastimes from the battle of Kurukṣetra.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Even though they could see him at other times, the gopīs were able to do this without restriction in front of others on the pretext of his leaving and entering Vraja (along with all others). The women of Mathurā speak like this since it is very possible that Kṛṣṇa will return there to remove their feelings of separation. We are most unfortunate since we cannot see him as described. The women spoke in this manner with fear, out of pain caused by prema (pra—bhāsamānāṣu), and could not stop speaking in this manner. The locative case indicates that this speech was befitting. Kṛṣṇa was able to reveal or hide his powers at will (yogeśvaraḥ). He also wanted to remove the burden of the earth (hariḥ). Thus it was natural that he desired to kill his opponent—an enemy with actual animosity (śatrum). And he was Bhagavān. Since you come in the dynasty of Bharata, you know such pastimes from the battle of Kurukṣetra.

    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    While the women still speak, the Lord makes up His mind — like a king who lets the petition finish before he signs the order. Their words were not in vain; the moment of decision has been waiting for their voice to bless it.

    Important Words
    • putra - sneha — affection for one's son
      Compound: putra (son) + sneha (affection). Vātsalya-rasa's exact name. The poet specifies the rasa even in the parents' grief — they suffer in the bhāva that makes them His parents.
    • śuca - āturau — afflicted with sorrow
      Compound: śuc (grief) + ātura (afflicted, sick). The same -ātura suffix used in the Bhāgavatam for the suffering of conditioned souls. Devakī and Vasudeva, though parents of Bhagavān, taste ordinary śoka — by His arrangement.
    • abudhau — the two who do not know
      Dual of a + budh (knowing). Not 'stupid' but 'not knowing' — specifically not knowing the strength (balam) of their sons. Vātsalya-rasa requires this abudhatā; if Yaśodā knew He was Bhagavān, she could not bind Him.
    • balam — strength
      From √bal. Specifically the strength of Bhagavān, which only Bhagavān knows. Cf. verse 41, where Balarāma's strength (the personification of bala) is shown lion-like. Even the parents of Bala-rāma do not know bala.
    Out of affection for the two Lords, Their parents [Devakī and Vasudeva] became overwhelmed with sorrow when they heard the women’s fearful statements. They grieved, not knowing their sons’ strength.
    Purport

    Naturally, Kṛṣṇa’s parents would lament in this situation, thinking “Why didn’t we keep our sons at home? Why did we allow Them to participate in this corrupt exhibition?”

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Out of affection for the two Lords, Their parents [Devakī and Vasudeva] became overwhelmed with sorrow when they heard the women's fearful statements. They grieved, not knowing their sons' strength.
    Commentary

    Krnsa’s parents, that is Vasudeva and Devaki, or the two fathers (pitarau) Vasudeva and Nanda, began to lament. "In departing from Vraja, why did we not instruct Akrura so that he would not bring Krsna and Balarama to the wrestling arena? Why are my sons in this irreligious city? Why were they not confined to my house? " In this way they regretted. Both mother and father were unaware of the strength of their sons. Out of parental affection, this knowledge became covered, and instead they felt remorse. There is the suggestion that this ignorance of their sons’ powers was temporary.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Another version has upaśrutvā. Hearing the words of the women, because they were close (upa) or hearing the words attentively, Vasudeva and Devakī identified themselves as his parents because of parental affection. Because of this, they saw them as their sons. Because of great sorrow they could not understand that their sons had great strength. They were not able to discern their power. They grieved. “Why was Akrūra not instructed from the beginning, when he was about to go to Vraja, so that this would not happen?” They lamented in retrospect. Abudhau can take the genitive or the accusative (balam). It is said kartṛ-karmaṇoḥ kṛti: possessive ending is after a word to demote the agent and the object, when used with a kṛt affix. (Pāṇini 2.3.65) An example is found in Bhaṭti-kavya: dhāyair āmodam uttamam (possessing the highest pleasure). The accusative case is used instead of the possessive.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Another version has upaśrutvā. Hearing the words of the women, because they were close (upa) or hearing the words attentively, Vasudeva and Devakī identified themselves as his parents because of parental affection. Because of this, they saw them as their sons. Because of great sorrow they could not understand that their sons had great strength. They were not able to discern their power. They grieved. “Why was Akrūra not instructed from the beginning, when he was about to go to Vraja, so that this would not happen?” They lamented in retrospect. Abudhau can take the genitive or the accusative (balam). It is said kartṛ-karmaṇoḥ kṛti: possessive ending is used after a word to denote the agent and the object, when used with a kṛt affix. (Pāṇini 2.3.65) An example is found in Bhaṭti-kavya: dhāyair āmodam uttamam (possessing the highest pleasure). The accusative case is used instead of the possessive.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Anahankarah (Freedom from False Ego)Tyaga (Renunciation / Letting Go)Bhakti (Devotion)
    Related verses
    BG 15.9 BG 18.64 BG 2.62 BG 3.11 BG 12.3 SB 4.29.2 BG 9.11 BG 7.8 BG 10.3 BG 13.1 BG 5.18 BG 6.1 BG 6.5 BG 1.1 BG 16.4 BG 1.41
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Vasudeva and Devakī, hearing the women's fearful description, fall into grief. They are still in chains; they do not know who their sons are. Their śoka is the most precious in the Bhāgavatam — the lament of parents who underestimate Bhagavān because they have loved Him as a son.

    Stories
    Yaśodā tying Kṛṣṇa with rope (SB 10.9)

    In the Dāmodara chapter, mother Yaśodā tries to bind the child Kṛṣṇa with rope; she succeeds only because He consents. She, like Devakī here, does not know His full strength — and that not-knowing is the very texture of vātsalya-rasa.

    Gauḍīyas treat Devakī's grief in this verse as a continuation of the same not-knowing. Vasudeva and Devakī know He is special; they do not yet know He is Bhagavān. Their śoka is the precondition of the awe that will arrive in verse 51.

    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.19

    ↗
    తైస్తైర్నియుద్ధవిధిభిర్వివిధైరచ్యుతేతరౌ । యుయుధాతే యథాన్యోన్యం తథైవ బలముష్టికౌ ॥ ౧౯ ॥

    Lord Balarāma and Muṣṭika .
    Important Words
    • niyuddha — wrestling, close combat
      Compound: ni (down, intensive) + yuddha (battle). Specifically grappling combat — fists and limbs, not weapons. The Bhāgavatam uses the precise term: the demons get the kind of fight they trained for.
    • vidhibhiḥ — by techniques
      Plural instrumental of vidhi. The wrestling traditions of the period had codified techniques — the verse acknowledges them. The Lord meets the demon inside the demon's own discipline.
    • acyuta - itarau — the two who are other than the infallible one
      Compound: acyuta (Kṛṣṇa) + itara (other). The poet names them by negation — Balarāma and Muṣṭika are 'the not-Kṛṣṇas'. Even in this minor verse, the chapter's center is Kṛṣṇa; everyone else is positioned in relation.
    • yathā — just as
      The śāstric correlative — paired with tathā eva in this verse. The grammar explicitly mirrors verses 2-5: Balarāma's fight is parallel to Kṛṣṇa's. The parallelism is doctrinal: Saṅkarṣaṇa shadows Vāsudeva move for move.
    Lord Balarāma and Muṣṭika, expertly displaying numerous wrestling techniques, battled each other in the same way that Lord Kṛṣṇa and His opponent did.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Lord Balarāma and Muṣṭika, expertly displaying numerous wrestling techniques, battled each other in the same way that Lord Kṛṣṇa and His opponent did.
    Commentary

    Just as Krsna and Canura (krsnetarau) fought with each, so Balarama fought with Mustika.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The fight is again described. They fought as explained previously. Taiḥ is repeated to show the astonishing nature of the fight. They fought similar to Kṛṣṇa and Cāṇūra. Thus Camera’s skill is indicated. There would be no thrill in the pastime if he were unqualified. The necessary actions were arranged by the līlā-śakti. Instead of ubhau cānura-keśavau sometimes vividhair acyutetarau is seen.

      His Grace Dina Bandhu Prabhu· 2023-08-05· English
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Balarāma and Muṣṭika lock in matched combat, expert against expert. The verse names the symmetry only to set up the asymmetry of the next two verses: the wrestler is at his ceiling, the Lord is not yet using His floor.

    Important Words
    • bhagavat - gātra — the limbs of the Supreme Lord
      Compound: bhagavat + gātra (limb). The Bhāgavatam never lets the Lord's body shrink into a mere fighting body — even mid-strike, it is named bhagavat-gātra. Gauḍīyas note: His limbs do not become demoniac instruments; the demon is destroyed by His sac-cid-ānanda body.
    • vajra - niṣpeṣa — crushing like a thunderbolt
      Compound: vajra (thunderbolt) + niṣpeṣa (grinding, pulverizing). The same vajra-vocabulary that verse 8 used for the wrestlers — now relocated to the Lord's limbs. The reversal is the verse's whole rhetorical action.
    • bhajyamāna — being broken
      Passive participle of √bhañj — to break. Phonetically close to bhajyamāna (being-worshiped) from √bhaj. The Bhāgavatam loves these near-homophones: the demon is bhajyamāna in one root while the gopīs are bhajyamānā in the other.
    • glānim — fatigue, exhaustion
      Accusative of glāni — decline, weariness. Cf. BG 4.7, yadā yadā hi dharmasya glāniḥ — when dharma's glāni appears, He appears. Here, glāni appears in the demon's body, which is the same thing in another grammar.
    The harsh blows from the Supreme Lord’s limbs fell like crushing lightning bolts upon Cāṇūra, breaking every part of his body and causing him more and more pain and fatigue.
    Purport

    Cāṇūra’s elbows, arms, knees and other limbs were all weakening.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The harsh blows from the Supreme Lord's limbs fell like crushing lightning bolts upon Cāṇūra, breaking every part of his body and causing him more and more pain and fatigue.
    Commentary

    By hard blows like crushing lightning bolts, Krsna injured the limbs of Canura.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Kṛṣṇa had inconceivable śakti (bhagavān). Because of this, Kṛṣṇa appeared as a thunderbolt among the wrestlers. Camera’s limbs broke because of the cruel crush of thunderbolts.

    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    The Lord's strikes fall on Cāṇūra like lightning bolts on a tree. Verse 8 had named the wrestlers vajra-bodied; now the actual vajra is on the other side. The grammar of hardness has reversed.

    Important Words
    • śyena - vegaḥ — with the speed of a hawk
      Compound: śyena (hawk) + vega (speed). Cf. verse 36, where Kaṁsa is also compared to a śyena. The Bhāgavatam keeps the bird-of-prey imagery for the demons — fast, predatory, but small under the Lord's hand.
    • muṣṭī kṛtya — having made the two fists
      Compound idiom: muṣṭi (fist) + kṛtya (having made). The grammar of preparation — Cāṇūra has to convert his hands into weapons. Kṛṣṇa's hand is always already a hand and never needs to be 'made into' a weapon.
    • vakṣasi — on the chest
      Locative of vakṣas. The chest where Śrīvatsa rests, where Lakṣmī lives. Cf. SB 10.83.41: vakṣaḥ-sthala-stha (resting on the chest). The demon strikes the seat of Śrī — and Śrī does not move.
    • abādhata — he struck (with intent to harm)
      Imperfect of √bādh — to oppress, harm. Stronger than mere 'hit'. The verb implies an attempt at injury; the next verse will show that the verb's intent is exhausted in the trying.
    Furious, Cāṇūra attacked Lord Vāsudeva with the speed of a hawk and struck His chest with both fists.
    Purport

    It appears that Cāṇūra, realizing he was being defeated, became furious and made a final attempt to defeat Lord Kṛṣṇa. The demon certainly had the spirit of a good fighter, but if he hoped for victory, he was certainly in the wrong place at the wrong time with the wrong person.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Furious, Cāṇūra attacked Lord Vāsudeva with the speed of a hawk and struck His chest with both fists.
    Commentary

    Canura struck (avadhata) on the chest.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    After becoming tired, Cāṇūra moved away, but then attacked with the speed of a hawk. Using both fists at once to strike Kṛṣṇa, Cāṇūra showed his great strength and his skill in fighting. Since Kṛṣṇa was the lord full of all powers (bhagavantam) Cāṇūra was foolish to attack him. Cāṇūra showed his cruel nature in attacking Vasudeva’s son (vāsudevam).

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    After becoming tired, Cāṇūra moved away, but then attacked with the speed of a hawk. Using both fists at once to strike Kṛṣṇa, Cāṇūra showed his great strength and his skill in fighting. Since Kṛṣṇa was the lord full of all powers (bhagavantam), Cāṇūra was foolish to attack him. Cāṇūra showed his cruel nature in attacking Vasudeva’s son (vāsudevam).

    Analogy

    Cāṇūra, furious, strikes the Lord's chest with both fists — striking the seat of Lakṣmī herself with bare hands. Like a man punching the sun, his arms are about to discover what they have already touched.

    Important Words
    • mālā - āhata — struck with a flower-garland
      Compound: mālā (garland) + āhata (struck). The simile of the elephant: a great bull is not even brushed by a flower. The strike that Cāṇūra meant as vajra (verse 20) is registered by the Lord as mālā. The asymmetry is total.
    • bhrāmayan — whirling
      Present participle of bhram. Cf. BG 18.61 (bhrāmayan sarva-bhūtāni) — the Lord whirls all beings on the wheel of māyā. Here, in literal grammar, He whirls Cāṇūra. The cosmic and the local meet in one verb.
    • indra - dhvajaḥ — Indra's flagpole
      Compound: indra (the king of gods) + dhvaja (flag, pole). The festival-pole erected for the rain-festival of Indra. Cf. SB 10.24-27 — the very festival Kṛṣṇa cancelled in Vraja, replacing Indra with Govardhana. The simile recalls that earlier victory.
    • kṣīṇa - jīvitam — whose life is exhausted
      Compound: kṣīṇa (diminished) + jīvita (life). The same kṣīṇa-vocabulary used for the dying world before Kalki. The Bhāgavatam's lexicon of ending is consistent across cantos.
    • visrasta - ākalpa — with scattered ornaments
      Compound: visrasta (loosened, fallen) + ākalpa (ornament, attire). His wrestler's regalia comes apart in the air. The Bhāgavatam keeps showing demoniac ornaments scattering at the moment of death — adornments that bhakti would have purified are now just litter.
    No more shaken by the demon’s mighty blows than an elephant struck with a flower garland, Lord Kṛṣṇa grabbed Cāṇūra by his arms, swung him around several times and hurled him onto the ground with great force. His clothes, hair and garland scattering, the wrestler fell down dead, like a huge festival column collapsing.
    Purport

    Śrīla Śrīdhara Svāmī explains the words indra-dhvaja as follows: “In Bengal, on the occasion of a certain festival, people erect a tall column in the form of a man and decorate it with flags, banners, etc. He [Cāṇūra] fell just as such a pole might fall.”

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    No more shaken by the demon's mighty blows than an elephant struck with a flower garland, Lord Kṛṣṇa grabbed Cāṇūra by his arms, swung him around several times and hurled him onto the ground with great force. His clothes, hair and garland scattering, the wrestler fell down dead, like a huge festival column collapsing.
    Commentary

    Indradvaja is famous in the eastern states.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Though he was the Lord, he began performing actions in order to give joy to his devotees who were suffering. He did not imitate being disturbed at all for obtaining pleasure of his own. Kṛṣṇa remained joyful, undisturbed like and elephant hit by many garlands. Though an elephant may be hit by many garlands, nothing happens to the elephant. Though Kṛṣṇa was struck many times by Cāṇūra with great force, nothing happened to Kṛṣṇa. Hari-vaṁśa describes this: yad ayaṁ bāhu-yuddhaṁ savairaṁ kartum udyataḥ / atra vai nigrahaḥ kāryas toṣayiṣyāmy ahaṁ jagat karūṣeṣu prasūto 'yaṁ cāṇūro nāma nāmataḥ / bāhu-yodhī sarīreṇa karmabhiś cānucintyatām // etena bahavo mallā nipātāntaram hatāḥ / raṅga-pratāpa-kāmena malla-mārgaś ca dūṣitaḥ // ye tu kecit sva-doṣeṇa rājṣaḥ paṇḍita-māninaḥ / pratāpārthe hatā mallā malla-hantur vadho hi saḥ // Since Cāṇūra is eager to wrestler with enmity, I must punish him. I will satisfy the world by this. This wrestler called Cāṇūra, born from the descendants of Karūṣa, is famous by his body and actions. It should be known that he has defeated and then killed many wrestlers and spoiled the sport of wrestling by his desire for fame in the arena. Some of the wrestlers have been killed in order to give glory to the foolish king because of his faulty behaviour. Killing the wrestlers was murder. Kṛṣṇa whirled him around many times and in a variety of ways. By the force of throwing him on the ground Cāṇūra died. His life merged into the ākāśa. bhrāmayitvā śata-guṇaṁ daitya-mallam amitrajit / bhūmāv āsphoṭayām āsa gagane gata-jīvitam // Whirling the demonic wrestler around a hundred times he splayed him out on the ground and making his life merge in the sky. He threw Cāṇūra on the ground, shaking him (pothayām āsa), as if giving him up, while saying “He is dead.” The word sa apatat (he fell) should be added to the comparison. He is compared to a flag staff falling because of his great size. Hari-vaṁśa says: dehena tasya mallasya cāṇurasya gatāyusaḥ sanniruddho mahā-raṅgaḥ sa śaileneva lakṣyate The large arena appeared to be obstructed by a mountain with the dead body of Cāṇūra.

      Her Grace Ratna Radhika Mataji· 2023-04-14· English
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Cāṇūra strikes the Lord's body, and the Lord is no more moved than an elephant struck with a flower garland. Then Kṛṣṇa grabs him, swings him through the air, and the wrestler falls like a festival flagpole. The flag of Kaṁsa's regime is taken down with the body of his own champion.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.24-25

    ↗
    తథైవ ముష్టిక: పూర్వం స్వముష్ట్యాభిహతేన వై । బలభద్రేణ బలినా తలేనాభిహతో భృశమ్ ॥ ౨౪ ॥ ప్రవేపిత: స రుధిరముద్వమన్ ముఖతోఽర్దిత: । వ్యసు: పపాతోర్వ్యుపస్థే వాతాహత ఇవాఙ్‍‍‍‍‍ఘ్రిప: ॥ ౨౫ ॥

    Important Words
    • talena — with the palm
      Instrumental of tala — the open palm. Balarāma does not need a fist; an open palm is enough. Cf. talāṅka — Balarāma's flag bears the palm-tree (tāla) emblem; the palm is His signature.
    • rudhiram udvaman — vomiting blood
      Compound clause: rudhira (blood) + ud + √vam (to vomit up). The classical Bhāgavata sign of fatal internal injury — used also of Pūtanā (SB 10.6) and of Kaṁsa's brothers (this chapter). Demoniac death has a consistent vocabulary.
    • aṅghripaḥ — a tree (lit. 'foot-drinker')
      Compound: aṅghri (foot, root) + pa (drinker). The Sanskrit poetic term for tree — that which drinks through its feet. The verse's simile (vāta-āhataḥ aṅghripaḥ — 'a tree felled by the wind') is the classical figure for sudden death: a tree that looked rooted, dropped by air. The wrestler's ground turns out to have been the Lord's tolerance.
    • balabhadreṇa — by Balabhadra (Balarāma)
      Name-compound: bala (strength) + bhadra (auspicious). The strength-giver, the auspicious-strong. The wrestler whose name is Muṣṭika (fist) is killed by the Lord whose name is Strength-Itself. The naming is theological.
    Similarly, Muṣṭika struck Lord Balabhadra with his fist and was slain. Receiving a violent blow from the mighty Lord’s palm, the demon trembled all over in great pain, vomited blood and then fell lifeless onto the ground, like a tree blown down by the wind.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Similarly, Muṣṭika struck Lord Balabhadra with his fist and was slain. Receiving a violent blow from the mighty Lord's palm, the demon trembled all over in great pain, vomited blood and then fell lifeless onto the ground, like a tree blown down by the wind.
    Commentary

    Balarama struch Mustika with his palm and mustka fell on the earth (uvi upasthe). Also Balarama crushed him with his embrace and uttered foul words as he lay dying.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Muṣṭika struck Balarāma with his fist, but was then struck by Balarāma’s strong palm. Balabhadram balocchrayāt: he was called Balarāma because of his great power. (SB 10.2.13) He was strong by nature. He struck Muṣṭika with the palm of his hand, which displayed that power (balinā). Cāṇūra was compared to a flag staff falling since he fell from high up. Muṣṭika fell while standing on the earth, and is thus compared to a tree falling over in the wind.

      His Holiness Bhakti Charu Swami· 2011-06-22· English
    • BCS SB 10-44-24-31 - 2011-06-22 New Vrindavan
      His Holiness Bhakti Charu Swami· 2011-06-22· English
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Muṣṭika strikes Balarāma with his fist, and Balarāma returns one open-palm slap — and the wrestler trembles, vomits blood, and falls like a tree in a high wind. The slap-and-tree image is the Bhāgavatam's wry way of saying: against Bhagavān's expansion, even the strongest wrestler is simply weather-struck timber.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.26

    ↗
    తత: కూటమనుప్రాప్తం రామ: ప్రహరతాం వర: । అవధీల్లీలయా రాజన్సావజ్ఞం వామముష్టినా ॥ ౨౬ ॥
    tataḥ kūṭam anuprāptaṁ
    [tatah kootam anupraaptam]
    rāmaḥ praharatāṁ varaḥ
    [raamah praharataam varah]
    avadhīl līlayā rājan
    [avadheel leelayaa raajan]
    sāvajñaṁ vāma-muṣṭinā
    [saavajnam vaama-mushtinaa]
    Word-for-word
    tataḥ — then kūṭam — the demonic wrestler Kūṭa anuprāptam — appearing on the scene rāmaḥ — Lord Balarāma praharatām — of fighters varaḥ — the best avadhīt — killed līlayā — playfully rājan — O King, Parīkṣit sa - avajñam — neglectfully vāma — left muṣṭinā — with His fist .

    with His fist .
    Important Words
    • līlayā — playfully
      Instrumental of līlā — pastime, play. The single most important word in Gauḍīya theology. Every act of Bhagavān is līlā: even killing a wrestler is play, not labor. The grammar of the verse encodes the doctrine.
    • sa - avajñam — with disregard
      Compound: sa (with) + avajñā (disregard, slighting). Balarāma kills without even granting Kūṭa the dignity of attention. Cf. BG 4.8 — paritrāṇāya sādhūnām — the same Lord whose face turns toward bhaktas turns barely an eye toward demons.
    • vāma - muṣṭinā — with the left fist
      Compound: vāma (left) + muṣṭi (fist). The left hand is the non-favored hand in classical contexts — Balarāma chooses His non-dominant side. The detail is the verse's whole point: this much is enough.
    • praharatām varaḥ — the best of those who strike
      Compound: praharatām (gen. plur. participle of pra + √hṛ, 'of those striking') + vara (best). Of all who strike, He is best — not by force but by economy. The Bhāgavatam pairs the descriptor with the left-fist detail: best, and barely trying.
    Confronted next by the wrestler Kūṭa, Lord Balarāma, the best of fighters, playfully and nonchalantly killed him with His left fist, O King.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Confronted next by the wrestler Kūṭa, Lord Balarāma, the best of fighters, playfully and nonchalantly killed him with His left fist, O King.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    He killed him effortlessly (līlayā) since he did by his left fist, and also without paying attention.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Balarāma killed Kūta, who approached him after Muṣṭika was killed (anu), or who approached him from behind out of fear. He is called Rāma because he gave pleasure to his friends. He did this without effort, using only his left fist, without much attention, since he was the best among fighters. O king! Śukadeva addressed Parīkṣit in joy, since the king glowing with joy. Or rājan can mean “Balarāma shone because of the pastime.”

      Her Grace Narayani Mataji· English
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Kūṭa rushes at Balarāma, and Balarāma kills him līlayā — playfully, casually — with the left fist, as a man flicks a fly. The right hand is not needed; the gaze is barely engaged. This is Bhagavān's signature: the catastrophic act done with the unbothered hand.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.27

    ↗

    Important Words
    • prapada - āhata — struck by the forepart of the foot
      Compound: prapada (the toe, the forepart of the foot) + āhata (struck). The toe — the lowest extremity — is enough. The Bhāgavatam keeps shrinking the killing-instrument: fist, palm, left fist, toe.
    • dvidhā vidīrnaḥ — torn in two
      Compound: dvidhā (in two) + vidīrṇa (split). Cf. SB 7.8.29-30 — Nṛsiṁha tearing Hiraṇyakaśipu. The vocabulary of demoniac tearing is consistent across Cantos: the demon split into pieces, the Lord intact.
    • śalaḥ — (the wrestler) Śala
      Proper name. Śalaḥ + tośalakaḥ — the verse pairs them and dispatches them in the same breath. The Bhāgavatam will not give villainous names ceremony; their music is curt.
    • nipetatuḥ — the two fell
      Dual perfect of ni + √pat. The same fall-verb that closed Cāṇūra's verse (apatat) and will close Kaṁsa's (papāta). The chapter is structured by repeated falling — the dharma-action is consistent across opponents.
    Then Kṛṣṇa kicked in Śala’s head and tore Tośala in half, and both wrestlers fell down dead.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Then Kṛṣṇa struck the wrestler Śala in the head with His toes and tore him in half. The Lord dealt with Tośala in the same way, and both wrestlers fell down dead.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Śala and Tośalaka, against the rules of wrestling, out of fear, attacked Kṛṣṇa together while he could not see, in order to grab Kṛṣṇa’s two legs. They approached with heads bowed.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Then Śala and Toṣala approached Kṛṣṇa. Seeing Kṛṣṇa’s great strength, the two did not want to approach Kṛṣṇa, but after Kūṭa approached Balarāma, out of fear of Kaṁsa they attacked Kṛṣṇa together. Or though they did not approach to fight like Kūṭa, they were killed since they were Kaṁsa’s followers standing nearby, ready to fight. Hi means certainly. Kṛṣṇa’s left foot hit his head, which fell off. Or the head shone (kaḥ) with crown and other ornaments. According to Viśva kaḥ can mean Brahmā, ātmā, fire or the sun. Kṛṣṇa was able to knock his head off with his foot from far away, attracting it with his foot (kṛṣṇa). They fell down completely, without life (nipetuḥ).

    Related values

    Concepts

    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Kṛṣṇa kicks Śala's head with His toe and tears Tośala in two — two more wrestlers gone in one verse, no commentary needed. The Bhāgavatam compresses the rest of the killing into the rhythm of a list: the slaughter has become arithmetic.

    Important Words
    • pradudruvuḥ — they fled
      Plural perfect of pra + √dru (to run). The verb of flight. The Bhāgavatam uses it for Kṛṣṇa's enemies again and again — when the front rank falls, the rest run. The doctrine: demoniac collectives have no internal cohesion.
    • prāṇa - parīpsavaḥ — desirous of saving their lives
      Compound: prāṇa (life-breath) + parīpsu (desiderative of √āp, 'wishing to obtain'). Lives become the only object once power has been broken. Gauḍīyas note: the demon, denied dominance, instantly becomes a mere body wanting to keep its breath.
    • śeṣāḥ — the remaining ones
      Plural of śeṣa — what is left over. The remnant. The five chiefs being named makes the unnamed simply 'remainders' — the Bhāgavatam will not catalogue cowards individually.
    • mallāḥ — the wrestlers
      Plural of malla. The class of professional wrestlers — paid champions of the king. The poet groups them by class as soon as they lose their leaders; demoniac identity disappears into anonymity once the head is removed.
    Cāṇūra, Muṣṭika, Kūṭa, Śala and Tośala having been killed, the remaining wrestlers all fled for their lives.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Cāṇūra, Muṣṭika, Kūṭa, Śala and Tośala having been killed, the remaining wrestlers all fled for their lives.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The wrestlers fled from fighting with Kṛṣṇa and from Kaṁsa for not fighting, out of fear for their lives.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The remaining wrestlers fled quickly. Why did they give fame and their fear of Kaṁsa? They desired to save their lives. They wanted to avoid death. They ignored everything else.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Anahankarah (Freedom from False Ego)
    Related verses
    BG 15.9 BG 9.1 BG 4.8 BG 10.1 BG 18.64 BG 15.7 BG 2.62 BG 3.11 BG 10.4 BG 12.3 BG 15.2 SB 4.29.2 BG 2.21 BG 16.1 BG 9.11 BG 3.30 BG 7.1
    Concepts
    Jiva
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    The five named wrestlers dead, the rest run for their lives — like soldiers who, the moment the standard-bearers fall, remember they are mortal. Demoniac courage was always a borrowed courage; when the borrowers fall, the lenders flee.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.29

    ↗
    గోపాన్వయస్యానాకృష్య తై: సంసృజ్య విజహ్రతు: । వాద్యమానేషు తూర్యేషు వల్గన్తౌ రుతనూపురౌ ॥ ౨౯ ॥
    gopān vayasyān ākṛṣya

    Important Words
    • vayasyān — age-mates, friends
      From vayas (age) — those of His same age. The technical sakhya-vocabulary: Kṛṣṇa's cowherd friends are vayasya, not 'devotees'. The relationship is structured by equality of age, not asymmetry of worship.
    • saṁsṛjya — having mingled with
      Gerund of sam + √sṛj. To pour together, to mix. The same root as visarga (creation by pouring forth). Kṛṣṇa pours Himself in among His friends — sakhya is pourable; aiśvarya is not.
    • valgantau — the two dancing
      Dual present participle of √valg (cf. verse 11, valgataḥ). The same dance-verb that the women used for His arena-footwork — now used for the post-victory dance with the gopas. The poet collapses the two dances into the same word.
    • ruta - nūpurau — with sounding ankle-bells
      Compound: ruta (sounding) + nūpura (ankle-bell). The same ankle-bells that the gopīs would hear from afar in Vraja. The Bhāgavatam reminds the listener of the sound of Vraja in the moment of Mathurā's liberation.
    Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma then called Their young cowherd boyfriends to join Them, and in their company the Lords danced about and sported, Their ankle bells resounding as musical instruments played.
    Purport

    Nowadays we see that in championship boxing matches, as soon as there is a victory, all the friends and relatives of the victorious boxer rush into the ring to congratulate him, and often the champion will dance about in great happiness. Exactly in this mood, Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma danced about, celebrating Their victory with Their friends and relatives.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma then called Their young cowherd boyfriends to join Them, and in their company the Lords danced about and sported, Their ankle bells resounding as musical instruments played.
    Commentary

    Krsna and Balarama began dancing around (valgantau) with their friends.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    They mocked the wrestlers with laughter and then called the cowherd boys. They were shy to enter suddenly, and thus were brought to the arena by force and all joined together, holding one another (saṁsṛjya). They jumped about (valgantau) like wrestlers. Though they were wearing cloth ornaments, they had jewelled ankle bells since these would not get crushed by the opponent in wrestling. They kept them because the sound nourished their jumping and incited their limbs to move. The two boys brought the other boys into the arena for fun and to make Kaṁsa angrier.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Out of fear of Kaṁsa or because of shyness the boys did not come, but he forcibly pulled them there and met them because they were friends. They played at fighting. The two boys moved here and there (valgantau) while their ankle bells sounded. They played in the huge arena to show the beauty of their play wrestling and to show the superiority of the cowherds over Cāṇūra and others and make Kaṁsa angry, in order to kill him. This would also counteract later suffering of himself and the boys (on separation). bala-kṣayaṁ vivṛddhiṁ ca dṛṣṭvā cāṇūra-kṛṣṇayoḥ / vārayāmāsa tūryāṇi kaṁsaḥ kopa-parāyaṇaḥ // mṛdaṅgādiṣu tūryeṣu pratiṣiddheṣu tat-kṣaṇāt / khe saṁgatāny avādyanta deva-tūryāṇy anekaśaḥ // jaya govinda cāṇūraṁ jahi keśava dānavam / antardhānagatā devās tam ūcur atiharṣitāḥ // Seeing the destruction of Cāṇūra’ strength and the increase of Kṛṣṇa’s strength, Kaṁsa, full of anger, stopped the music. When the mṛdaṅgas and other instruments stopped, at that moment in the sky the devatas’ many instruments began to sound together. The devatās spoke in joy in the sky “Victory to Govinda! Keśava! Kill the demon Cāṇūra.” Viṣṇu Purāṇa Hari-vaṁśa says: tataḥ prasvinna-vadanaḥ kṛṣṇa-praṇihitekṣaṇaḥ / nyavārayata tūryāṇi kaṁsaḥ savyena pāṇinā // With sweating face, Kaṁsa glared at Kṛṣṇa and stopped the music with his left hand. svayam eva pravādyanta tūryaghoṣāś ca sarvaśaḥ All the instruments began playing on their own.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Dana (Charity / Generosity)Asteya (Non-stealing / Honesty)Aparigraha (Non-possessiveness)Seva (Service / Selfless Action)Yajña (Sacrifice / Spirit of Offering)Karuna (Kindness / Compassion)Santosha (Contentment)Discriminative Wisdom
    Related verses
    BG 17.20 BG 16.1 BG 7.1 BG 8.2 BG 17.4 BG 13.8 BG 8.1 BG 18.1 BG 12.6
    Concepts
    Jiva
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    The wrestlers dead, Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma call Their cowherd friends and dance — ankle-bells ringing, musical instruments still playing, the killing already turning into a Vraja-style village festival. The arena becomes a courtyard. Only Vraja-rasa can absorb violence this fast.

    Stories
    The rāsa-līlā with the cowherd boys

    In Vraja, before all this, Kṛṣṇa would dance with His sakhā cowherd boys in the forest — ankle bells ringing, flute in hand, instruments echoing off Govardhana. The boys never doubted Him; their friendship needed no proof.

    Now, in the Mathurā arena, with five wrestlers dead at His feet, He immediately calls those same boys forward and dances with them again. Gauḍīyas read this as a doctrinal statement: even in the most aiśvarya-heavy scene (demon-killing), He instantly reverts to sakhya-rasa as soon as His friends are present. The friends are the rasa.

    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.30

    ↗
    జనా: ప్రజహృషు: సర్వే కర్మణా రామకృష్ణయో: । ఋతే కంసం విప్రముఖ్యా: సాధవ: సాధు సాధ్వితి ॥ ౩౦ ॥

    Important Words
    • ṛte kaṁsam — except Kaṁsa
      Indeclinable ṛte (except) + accusative of Kaṁsa. The whole world rejoices; one man does not. The Bhāgavatam loves this isolation-syntax — one against the chorus is always the demon's grammar (cf. SB 7, Hiraṇyakaśipu against all).
    • vipra - mukhyāḥ — the chief brāhmaṇas
      Compound: vipra (brāhmaṇa) + mukhya (chief). The brāhmaṇa class, kept neutral so far in the arena, now publicly endorses. Gauḍīyas note: brāhmaṇa endorsement is dharma's seal — the regime is now officially without legitimacy.
    • sādhu sādhu iti — 'Excellent! Excellent!'
      The vocative-style cry. The same sādhu-sādhu the Bhāgavatam records at Prahlāda's victory, at Dhruva's coronation, at Bhīṣma's death — the chorus of approving sages. The same chorus now sounds in Mathurā.
    • prajahṛṣuḥ — they rejoiced
      Plural perfect of pra + √hṛṣ (to thrill). Specifically the bristling-of-the-hair joy — sāttvika-bhāva of romāñca. The whole arena exhibits a sāttvika-bhāva that one man (Kaṁsa) is constitutionally unable to feel.
    Everyone except Kaṁsa rejoiced at the wonderful feat Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma had performed. The exalted brāhmaṇas and great saints exclaimed, “Excellent! Excellent!”
    Purport

    It is understood that as the best of the brāhmaṇas and saints were exclaiming “Excellent! Excellent!” the worst of the brāhmaṇas, namely Kaṁsa’s priests, were seriously grieving.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Everyone except Kaṁsa rejoiced at the wonderful feat Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma had performed. The exalted brāhmaṇas and great saints exclaimed, "Excellent! Excellent!"
    Commentary

    Among the brahmanas those who were chief, and were devotees, shouted, "This is very good!" The low class brahmanas who were priests of Kamsa cried out, "Oh what misfortune!"

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The devotees and the chief brāhmaṇas praised him because they lost all fear of Kaṁsa on realizing Kṛṣṇa’s greatness. They rejoiced by clearly saying “This is very good!”

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Everyone was filled with intense joy. Balarāma gave pleasure to the people (rāma). Kṛṣṇa attracted everyone’s hearts or gave everyone the highest bliss. They were blissful because of their activities of killing the demons. Balarāma is mentioned first because he was elder. The kṣatriyas and vaiśyas etc. among whom there were the chief brāhmaṇas, all being devotees (sādhavaḥ), sang. Or the best of the brāhmaṇas and the other devotees praised them since other brāhmaṇas were afraid of Kaṁsa and thus were not so bold.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Svadhyaya (Self-study & Scripture Study)Samata (Equanimity / Even-mindedness)Nishkama Karma (Selfless Action)
    Related verses
    BG 4.8 BG 15.7 BG 3.11 BG 15.2 BG 16.1 BG 3.19 BG 3.30 BG 2.66 BG 12.5 BG 6.4 BG 13.8 BG 7.8 BG 3.21 BG 2.12
    Concepts
    JivaKarma
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Everyone except Kaṁsa rejoices — brāhmaṇas, sādhus, citizens calling out 'sādhu sādhu'. The chorus has shifted; the one face that does not move is the king's. A regime that cannot rejoice in dharma's victory has already announced its end.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.31

    హతేషు మల్లవర్యేషు విద్రుతేషు చ భోజరాట్ । న్యవారయత్ స్వతూర్యాణి వాక్యం చేదమువాచ హ ॥ ౩౧ ॥

    Important Words
    • bhoja - rāṭ — the Bhoja king
      Compound: bhoja (the Bhoja dynasty) + rāj (king). Kaṁsa is named by dynasty, not by personal name — the Bhāgavatam reduces him to office. He is about to die; he has already lost his name.
    • nyavārayat — he stopped
      Imperfect of ni + √vṛ (to cover, restrain). The verb of suppression — the king stops music the way he had stopped truth. Cf. verse 32: he will now try to stop persons too. The verb generalizes.
    • tūryāṇi — the musical instruments
      Plural of tūrya — drums, conches, the loud instruments of public ceremony. The same tūryas resound in verse 42 from the demigods; the music doesn't disappear, it relocates. Kaṁsa silences his court; the heavens take over.
    • uvāca ha — indeed said
      Idiomatic: uvāca (he said) + ha (emphatic, surely). The narrative-emphatic — the Bhāgavatam underlines the moment because what follows is Kaṁsa's last full speech. Listen carefully; he will not get another.
    The Bhoja king, seeing that his best wrestlers had all been killed or had fled, stopped the musical performance originally meant for his pleasure and spoke the following words.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The Bhoja king, seeing that his best wrestlers had all been killed or had fled, stopped the musical performance originally meant for his pleasure and spoke the following words.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    He stopped the music which he had commissioned (sva-tūryānī), not the devatā music which he could not stop since he had lost his powers now. Or he could not stop the music which played by itself. Sometimes sa is seen instead of sva. Viṣṇu Purāṇa explains that the music stopped previously. bala-kṣayaṁ vivṛddhiṁ ca dṛṣṭvā cāṇūra-kṛṣṇayoḥ / vārayāmāsa tūryāṇi kaṁsaḥ kopa-parāyaṇaḥ // mṛdaṅgādiṣu tūryeṣu pratiṣiddheṣu tat-kṣaṇāt / khe saṁgatāny avādyanta deva-tūryāṇy anekaśaḥ // jaya govinda cāṇūraṁ jahi keśava dānavam / antardhānagatā devās tam ūcur atiharṣitāḥ // Seeing the destruction of Cāṇūra’ strength and the increase of Kṛṣṇa’s strength, Kaṁsa, full of anger stopped the music. When the mṛdaṅgas and other instruments stopped, at that moment in the sky the devatas’ many instruments began to sound together. The devatās spoke in joy in the sky “Victory to Govinda! Keśava! Kill the demon Cāṇūra.” Hari-vaṁśa says: tataḥ prasvinna-vadanaḥ kṛṣṇa-praṇihitekṣaṇaḥ / nyavārayata tūryāṇi kaṁsaḥ savyena pāṇinā // With sweating face, Kaṁsa glared at Kṛṣṇa and stopped the music with his left hand. svayam eva pravādyanta tūryaghoṣāś ca sarvaśaḥ All the instruments began playing on their own. This means music again sounded though Kaṁsa stopped it. He stopped it on the pretext of speaking so that his words could be heard. He spoke clearly (ha). Kaṁsa was king of some people of the Bhoja dynasty, a branch of the Yadus, but now, everyone held him in disrespect.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    His best wrestlers had been killed. Others had quickly fled in fear far away. He stopped his instruments but could not stop the heavenly instruments, according to Viṣṇu Purāṇa. Or according to Śukadeva, the devatās out of fear of Kaṁsa did not have instruments. Kaṁsa alone had instruments to inspire his wrestlers with a desire for victory. Because his desire was thwarted, he stopped the music. He revealed his wicked nature by his words. He spoke clearly (ha) so that everyone could hear his instructions. Actually he spoke with internal suffering. He informed the Bhojas who were his followers. He ruled because of some portion of the Yadus called Bhojas (bhoja-rāṭ).

    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Kaṁsa cuts off the music. The wrestlers were entertainment; with their bodies down, the songs are pointless. The verse marks the exact second a tyrant's spectacle becomes mere violence — the music falls silent before the king does.

    Important Words
    • niḥsārayata — drive out
      Imperative plural of niḥ + √sṛ (causal: to cause to go out). The verb of exile — used for those who 'cause others to go out'. Ironically, Kaṁsa is the one who is about to be 'caused to go out' of his throne and body.
    • durvṛttau — the two wicked-acting (boys)
      Dual of durvṛtta — ill-conducted. Kaṁsa labels Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma wicked. The Bhāgavatam preserves the slander as documentary evidence; the demon's last vocabulary is recorded so dharma may catalogue what it overcame.
    • harata — seize
      Imperative plural of √hṛ. The demoniac use of hara — to take by force. Cf. verse 38 where Kṛṣṇa is Hari (the one who takes the demon). Two flavors of the same root: taking-for-greed and taking-for-mercy.
    • durmatim — fool, evil-minded one
      Compound: dur (bad) + mati (mind). Used by Kaṁsa of Nanda. The Bhāgavatam keeps the demon's slander accurate-as-record; Gauḍīyas note that Nanda is in fact the topmost vātsalya-bhakta, and Kaṁsa's word names the opposite of what it points at.
    [Kaṁsa said:] Drive the two wicked sons of Vasudeva out of the city! Confiscate the cowherds’ property and arrest that fool Nanda!
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    [Kaṁsa said:] Drive the two wicked sons of Vasudeva out of the city! Confiscate the cowherds' property and arrest that fool Nanda!
    Commentary

    "Drive out the two wicked sons of Vasudeva" can also mean "Give these boys of unattainable qualities the best position in the city. Protect Krsna, the treasure of the cowherd men. Tie up Nanda whose intelligence is perfect with ropes of affection. Establish affectionate relationship with him."

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The two boys were evil because they killed the wrestlers in the matches. Others should fight with the two since they are sons of Vasudeva. Since no one can defeat them, they should be driven from the city. The statement also confirms that the two boys had greater attraction for the forests as dwellers in the forest. They were his nephews. Nanda is considered evil since he had friendship with Vasudeva. Out of ignorance Nanda is protecting my enemies. Capture him but do not kill him.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Drive the boys from our city since they are evil, having killed the wrestlers while playing. This is reasonable since they are sons of Vasudeva. They are similar to their father. The real meaning however is that they have proper behaviour, being merciful even to the wicked (durvṛttau) since they are Vasudeva’s sons. Forcibly seize the dairy products brought by the cowherds. Making many offerings at once can cause problems for us. Bind Nanda with chains because he is wicked. Secretly he protected my enemy. The real meanings is “He is merciful even to the wicked.” Nanda-gopaś ca durmedhā pāpeṣv abhirato mama Āyaṣair nigaḍākārair loha-pāśāir nigṛhyatām Crafty Nanda is absorbed in sins. He should be bound with iron chains. Hari-vaṁsa Nando ‘pi gṛyatāṁ pāpo nigaḍair āyaṣair iha Sinful Nanda should be bound with iron chains. Viṣṇu Purāṇa He gave this instruction because he was afraid of the cowherds. Or naturally bad words came from his mouth in order to cause danger to best of the devotees.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Anahankarah (Freedom from False Ego)Kshama (Forgiveness / Patience)Aparigraha (Non-possessiveness)Karuna (Kindness / Compassion)
    Related verses
    BG 15.9 BG 12.13 BG 18.64 BG 2.62 BG 3.11 BG 12.3 SB 4.29.2 BG 9.11 BG 7.8 BG 10.3 BG 13.1 BG 5.18 BG 6.1 BG 6.5 BG 1.1
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Drive out the sons of Vasudeva, confiscate the cowherds' property, arrest Nanda — Kaṁsa, beaten, falls back on bureaucratic violence. When demoniac power loses the field, it always retreats to paperwork. The decree is barked at the air.

    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.33

    ↗
    వసుదేవస్తు దుర్మేధా హన్యతామాశ్వసత్తమ: । ఉగ్రసేన: పితా చాపి సానుగ: పరపక్షగ: ॥ ౩౩ ॥
    vasudevas tu durmedhā
    [vasudevas tu durmedhaa]
    hanyatām āśv asattamaḥ
    [hanyataam aashv asattamah]
    ugrasenaḥ pitā cāpi
    [ugrasenah pitaa chaapi]
    sānugaḥ para-pakṣa-gaḥ
    [saanugah para-paksha-gah]
    Word-for-word
    vasudevaḥ — Vasudeva tu — and furthermore durmedhā — the foolish-minded hanyatām — should be killed āśu — immediately asat - tamaḥ — the worst of the impure ugrasenaḥ — Ugrasena pitā — my father ca api — also sa — together with anugaḥ — his followers para — of the enemy pakṣa - gaḥ — taking the side .

    Important Words
    • ugrasenaḥ pitā — Ugrasena, my father
      The naming is the horror. Kaṁsa specifies the relationship — 'my father' — and then says hanyatām. The Bhāgavatam keeps the relational word in place to highlight the rupture: even speaking pitā does not stop him.
    • para - pakṣa - gaḥ — siding with the enemy
      Compound: para (other) + pakṣa (side) + ga (going). The demoniac's test: anyone not on his side is automatically the enemy's side. Cf. BG 16's enumeration of asuric traits — the bifurcating mind that knows only mine and enemy.
    • asat - tamaḥ — most untrue
      Compound: asat (untrue, unreal) + superlative tama. Kaṁsa calls Vasudeva 'most untrue'; the Bhāgavatam quietly registers that asat is in fact the demon's own category (asad-grāha, the unreal-grasp, BG 16). The slander is auto-descriptive.
    • durmedhā — of bad understanding
      Compound: dur + medhā (intellect). The demoniac will-to-misname. Cf. SB 7.5.30 — Hiraṇyakaśipu calls Prahlāda mūḍha (fool). The same root-pattern: the demon's last act is to mislabel the wise.
    Kill that most evil fool Vasudeva! And also kill my father, Ugrasena, along with his followers, who have all sided with our enemies!
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Kill that most evil fool Vasudeva! And also kill my father, Ugrasena, along with his followers, who have all sided with our enemies!
    Commentary

    This verse can also mean something else. "Everyone should take shelter of Vasudeva who has unattainable intelligence, who has no superior . (han means to go as well as to kill). Also take shelter of Ugrasena who has taken shelter of the supreme lord (para paksa gah)."

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Vasudeva is wicked (sudurmedhā) because he switched his son for Nanda’s child. He is most untruthful (asattamaḥ) since he broke his promise to deliver all his sons to me. Sometimes vasudevas tu durmedhā is seen. The word ca indicates all those mentioned. Though Vasudeva is just his father, he should still be killed. The followers of Vasudeva and Ugrasena should also be killed since they are loyal to them. There is another meaning to his statement however. Display the two sons, whose natures are hard to understand (durvṛttau—previous verse) in Mathurā. Bring Kṛṣṇa, the treasure of the cowherds, to this city and protect him. “Nanda will not give up Kṛṣṇa.” Keep grieving (durmatim) Nanda here, delaying him by means of Kṛṣṇa. Bring Vasudeva, whose intelligence is hard to understand. Everyone should take shelter of him. There is no one equal to him in purity (asattamaḥ). Take shelter of Ugrasena and his followers, who are loyal to the Lord (para).

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Vasudeva is wicked (sudurmedhā) because he switched his son for Nanda’s child. He is most untruthful (asattamaḥ) since he broke his promise to deliver all his sons to me. The real meaning of sudurmedhā is “His mind is glorious, showing affection even to the wicked.” There is no purer person than him (asattamaḥ). The word ca indicates all those mentioned. Though Ugrasena is his father, he should still be killed since he is partial to my enemies or follows Vasudeva. The followers of Vasudeva and Ugrasena should also be killed since they are loyal to them. There is another meaning to his statement however. Pitā means he was born from Ugrasena. However Hari-vaṁśa says that Kaṁsa’s real father was a Dānava named Drumila, who by trick seduced Ugrasena’s wife. But Kaṁsa did not know, and thus Śukadeva follows his ignorance in saying that Ugrasena was his father.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Anahankarah (Freedom from False Ego)Apaishunam (Non-slander)Kshama (Forgiveness / Patience)Asteya (Non-stealing / Honesty)Aparigraha (Non-possessiveness)
    Related verses
    BG 15.9 BG 17.14 BG 17.20 BG 18.64 BG 2.62 BG 3.11 BG 12.3 SB 4.29.2 BG 9.11 BG 8.2 BG 17.4 BG 7.8 BG 10.3 BG 13.1
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Kaṁsa orders the killing of his own father (Ugrasena) and of Vasudeva — the demon's last instinct is to widen the violence inward, against his own kin. Tyrannies always eat themselves last. He is naming his own end aloud and does not hear it.

    Stories
    Hiraṇyakaśipu against his own son

    Hiraṇyakaśipu attempts to kill his five-year-old son Prahlāda through poison, fire, snakes, elephants, weapons — all because the boy will not stop chanting Hari (SB 7.5-8). The Bhāgavatam treats this as the diagnostic of asuric power: it turns inward on its own family.

    Kaṁsa here repeats the pattern. He orders the death of his own father Ugrasena, just as Hiraṇyakaśipu orders the death of his own son. Gauḍīyas read both episodes as one teaching: the demoniac, when cornered, cannot stop at strangers. The kin are next.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.34

    ↗
    ఏవం వికత్థమానే వై కంసే ప్రకుపితోఽవ్యయ: । లఘిమ్నోత్పత్య తరసా మఞ్చముత్తుఙ్గమారుహత్ ॥ ౩౪ ॥
    evaṁ vikatthamāne vai

    Important Words
    • vikatthamāne — while (he was) boasting
      Locative present participle of vi + √katth — to brag. The exact verb the Bhāgavatam uses of demons in their final speeches — Hiraṇyakaśipu, Rāvaṇa, now Kaṁsa. Demoniac courage exhausts itself as words just before the body fails.
    • avyayaḥ — the imperishable one
      From a (not) + vyaya (decline, decay). One of the Lord's standard epithets — He does not diminish. The Bhāgavatam places it next to laghimnā: He who never diminishes shows His lightness.
    • laghimnā — by laghimā (the power of becoming light)
      Instrumental of laghimā — one of the eight mystic siddhis. The Lord uses the wrestler's discipline (the eight siddhis are also yogi's) to leap onto the dais. The grammar: He owns the powers His enemies imitate.
    • mañcam-uttuṅgam — the high dais
      Compound: mañca (platform) + uttuṅga (lofty). The same platform from which Kaṁsa has watched the wrestling. The Bhāgavatam loves spatial inversions: the watcher becomes the watched, the throne becomes the killing floor.
    As Kaṁsa thus raved so audaciously, the infallible Lord Kṛṣṇa, intensely angry, quickly and easily jumped up onto the high royal dais.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    As Kaṁsa thus raved so audaciously, the infallible Lord Kṛṣṇa, intensely angry, quickly and easily jumped up onto the high royal dais.
    Commentary

    Hearing the offense to his devotees, Krsna who is without injury (avyaya), unlike all the jivas, became very angry (prakupitah). He jumped so quickly on the king’s platform that no one saw him do it.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    His babbling consisted of criticizing others and praising himself. Since Kṛṣṇa was famous as being inexhaustible (avyayaḥ), he did not become exhausted by leaping on the stage, so quickly that no one could see it (laghimnā). With great impetus (tarasā) he ascended the platform. Aruhat should be arohat.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Vai means certainly. Kṛṣṇa became very angry at the proud words of Kaṁsa which were more and more offensive. Quickly he jumped on the platform though it was high.

    • BKG SB 10-44-34 - Vaisnava Aparadha makes Krsna angry - 2024-05-01 Lautoka
      His Holiness Bir Krishna Goswami· 2024-05-01· English
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    As Kaṁsa rants, Kṛṣṇa leaps onto the high royal dais — laghimnā, with the lightness of mystic power. The grammar of avyaya (infallible) and laghima (lightness) makes the leap weightless. He does not climb the throne; He arrives on it.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.35

    ↗
    తమావిశన్తమాలోక్య మృత్యుమాత్మన ఆసనాత్ । మనస్వీ సహసోత్థాయ జగృహే సోఽసిచర్మణీ ॥ ౩౫ ॥

    Important Words
    • mṛtyum — death personified
      Accusative of mṛtyu. Kaṁsa correctly identifies what is approaching — the Bhāgavatam credits him with one moment of accurate vision. Cf. BG 10.34 (mṛtyuḥ sarva-haraś cāham). The vision is right; the response is too late.
    • manasvī — quick-witted
      From manas — possessing presence-of-mind. The Bhāgavatam will not deny Kaṁsa his cleverness; demons in this Purāṇa are often acute. What they lack is not intellect but surrender.
    • asi - carmaṇī — sword and shield
      Compound dual: asi (sword) + carman (shield, hide). The kṣatriya's pair. Kaṁsa dies armed. The Bhāgavatam shows him taking up the right equipment for the wrong purpose.
    • ālokya — seeing
      Gerund of ā + √lok. The act of seeing — the very faculty Kaṁsa has cultivated through fear (cf. verse 39). His whole life has been ālokana of the future-Kṛṣṇa; in this moment, the seen becomes the present.
    Seeing Lord Kṛṣṇa approaching like death personified, the quick-witted Kaṁsa instantly rose from his seat and took up his sword and shield.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Seeing Lord Kṛṣṇa approaching like death personified, the quick-witted Kaṁsa instantly rose from his seat and took up his sword and shield.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Seeing Kṛṣṇa quickly enter the royal pavilion, seeing him as his own death, out of great fear, fearful Kaṁsa (manasvī -having a bad mind) rose from his seat and, not having a weapon, grabbed his sword and shield. bhūma-nindā-praśaṁsāsu nitya-yoge’tiśāyane | saṁsarge’sti vivakṣāyāṁ bhavanti matub-ādayaḥ || iti | Words ending in mat or other suffixes express abundance, criticism, praise, constant connection, excess, or combination. Thus manasvin can mean “having abundance” of mental anguish or fear.

    Related values

    BG 15.9 BG 12.13 BG 18.64 BG 2.62 BG 3.11 BG 12.3 SB 4.29.2 BG 9.11 BG 7.8 BG 10.3 BG 13.1 BG 5.18 BG 6.5 BG 1.1 BG 16.4
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Kaṁsa sees the Lord approaching like Death itself (mṛtyu) and instantly rises — sword and shield in hand. The Bhāgavatam grants him the last competence of a kṣatriya: he dies with weapons drawn, not hiding. Even the demon's death has a kind of dignity that bhakti respects.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.36

    ↗
    తం ఖడ్‌గపాణిం విచరన్తమాశుశ్యేనం యథా దక్షిణసవ్యమమ్బరే । సమగ్రహీద్ దుర్విషహోగ్రతేజాయథోరగం తార్క్ష్యసుత: ప్రసహ్య ॥ ౩౬ ॥
    taṁ khaḍga-pāṇiṁ vicarantam āśu
    [tam khadga-paanim vicharantam aashu]
    śyenaṁ yathā dakṣiṇa-savyam ambare
    [shyenam yathaa dakshina-savyam ambare]

    Important Words
    • tārkṣya - sutaḥ — son of Tārkṣya (Garuḍa)
      Compound: tārkṣya (the sage / Garuḍa's father) + suta (son). Garuḍa — Viṣṇu's eternal carrier. The verse invokes him to describe Kṛṣṇa's seizure: Kṛṣṇa moves like His own mount, the Lord borrows the gesture of His own servant.
    • uragam — a serpent
      From ura (chest) + ga (going) — 'chest-goer', one who moves on the chest. The classical Sanskrit word for snake. Kaṁsa, in the verse's typology, is the chest-goer; Kṛṣṇa is the sky-goer.
    • durviṣaha - ugra - tejāḥ — of intolerable, fierce splendor
      Compound: durviṣaha (unbearable) + ugra (fierce) + tejas (radiance). Three intensifiers stacked. The Bhāgavatam underlines that what Kaṁsa faces is not a wrestler but Bhagavān's tejas — the demon would not survive even staring.
    • samagrahīt — He seized completely
      Imperfect of sam + √grah. The total grasp. Cf. SB 10.6 (Pūtanā's seizure) and SB 10.4 (Kaṁsa seizing the infant Devakī's daughter). The chapter is closing the loop: the seizer is finally seized.
    Sword in hand, Kaṁsa moved quickly from side to side like a hawk in the sky. But Lord Kṛṣṇa, whose fearsome strength is irresistible, powerfully seized the demon just as the son of Tārkṣya might capture a snake.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Sword in hand, Kaṁsa moved quickly from side to side like a hawk in the sky. But Lord Kṛṣṇa, whose fearsome strength is irresistible, powerfully seized the demon just as the son of Tārkṣya might capture a snake.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Kaṁsa moved about quickly in various ways (vi—carantam), circling around, to the left and right with great skill, like a hawk in the sky. This indicates that he was hard to catch. But Kṛṣṇa seized him with great strength (prasahya) since he was endowed with great, intolerable ferocity (durviṣahogra-tejā), just as Garuḍa attacks intolerably ferocious snakes. The example shows that many intolerably ferocious snakes can be easily caught since their power is ordinary compared to Kṛṣṇa’s.

    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Kaṁsa darts side to side like a hawk in the sky; Kṛṣṇa seizes him as Garuḍa seizes a snake. The bird-of-prey simile that the Bhāgavatam gave Cāṇūra (verse 21) is now applied to Kaṁsa — and immediately swallowed by the bigger bird. The hawk meets Garuḍa.

    Stories
    Garuḍa's nature

    Garuḍa is Viṣṇu's mount and the eternal enemy of serpents — in the Mahābhārata's Ādi-parva, his birth-song is the song of snakes scattering. Wherever Garuḍa lands, the snake-tribes flee or are eaten.

    The Bhāgavatam invokes Garuḍa here as the natural figure for Kṛṣṇa's seizure of Kaṁsa. The demon is named a snake-equivalent; the Lord is named tārkṣya-sutaḥ (Garuḍa). Gauḍīyas note: this is not random simile — the verse is identifying Kaṁsa typologically with the sarpa-class of obstacles, the residual ahaṅkāra that bhakti must finally devour.

    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.37

    ↗
    ప్రగృహ్య కేశేషు చలత్కిరీటంనిపాత్య రఙ్గోపరి తుఙ్గమఞ్చాత్ । తస్యోపరిష్టాత్ స్వయమబ్జనాభ:పపాత విశ్వాశ్రయ ఆత్మతన్త్ర: ॥ ౩౭ ॥
    pragṛhya keśeṣu calat-kirītaṁ

    Important Words
    • keśeṣu — by the hair
      Locative plural of keśa. Cf. SB 1.16-17 — the Mahābhārata's Draupadī-by-the-hair, which the Bhāgavatam never lets the listener forget. Pulling by the hair is the gesture of total dishonor; in this verse it is finally turned against the demon.
    • calat - kirīṭam — with the crown shaking-off
      Compound: calat (moving, falling) + kirīṭa (crown). The crown is in motion — already separating. The Bhāgavatam describes the dynasty's symbolic object as in mid-fall before the body has landed.
    • abja - nābhaḥ — the lotus-naveled
      Compound: abja (lotus) + nābhi (navel). The cosmic epithet — the navel from which Brahmā arose on a lotus. The Bhāgavatam places this epithet exactly at the moment Kṛṣṇa drops onto Kaṁsa: the universe-navel descends on the demon's body.
    • ātma - tantraḥ — self-dependent
      Compound: ātma (self) + tantra (depending-upon). The technical word for absolute autonomy. Bhagavān depends on no one; the verse names this immediately after naming Him viśva-āśraya (refuge of the universe). He is the refuge that needs no refuge.
    • viśva - āśrayaḥ — shelter of the universe
      Compound: viśva (universe) + āśraya (shelter). The very being who is now killing one demon also shelters all worlds. Gauḍīyas note: His killing of demons is itself a mode of His shelter — the same hand secures the bhakta and removes the threat.
    Grabbing Kaṁsa by the hair and knocking off his crown, the lotus-naveled Lord threw him off the elevated dais onto the wrestling mat. Then the independent Lord, the support of the entire universe, threw Himself upon the King.
    Purport

    In Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Śrīla Prabhupāda describes the death of Kaṁsa as follows: “Kṛṣṇa at once straddled his chest and began to strike him over and over again. Simply from the strokes of His fist, Kaṁsa lost his vital force.”

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Grabbing Kaṁsa by the hair and knocking off his crown, the lotus-naveled Lord threw him off the elevated dais onto the wrestling mat. Then the independent Lord, the support of the entire universe, threw Himself upon the King.
    Commentary

    Krsna threw himself upon the fallen Kamsa, so that his body would not be injured by hitting the ground. Krsna wanting to kill Kamsa, at that moment thought of himself as the shelter of the universe, which was burdened by Kamsa (visvasraya).

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This verse shows how he caught Kaṁsa. He held him firmly by his hair. “How could he suddenly catch him by the hair since his hair was bound by a crown?” His crown fell off when he moved his head violently, as Kṛṣṇa attempted to grab his head. Viṣṇu Purāṇa says keśeṣvākṛṣya vigalat kirīṭam: when Kṛṣṇa grabbed Kaṁsa’s hair, Kaṁsa’s crown fell off. Hari-vaṁśa says: mukuṭaś cāpata tasya kāṣcano vajra-bhūṣitaḥ śirasas tasya kṛṣṇena parāmṛṣṭasya pāṇinā When Kṛṣṇa grabbed him with his hand, the crown made of gold and ornamented with diamonds fell off his head. Kṛṣṇa was independent because he was the shelter of the universe. The universe is within the lotus of the navel (abja-nābhaḥ) of Garbhodakaśāyī Viṣṇu, who is but an expansion of Kṛṣṇa.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Anahankarah (Freedom from False Ego)
    Related verses
    BG 15.9 BG 9.1 BG 4.8 BG 10.1 BG 18.64 BG 15.7 BG 2.62 BG 3.11 BG 10.4 BG 12.3 BG 15.2 SB 4.29.2 BG 2.21 BG 16.1 BG 9.11 BG 3.30 BG 7.1
    Concepts
    Jiva
    Personal Guidance
    Analogy

    Kṛṣṇa grabs Kaṁsa by the hair, knocks off the crown, and throws him from the dais onto the wrestling floor. Then He throws Himself on top. The crown — the central object of demoniac fear — falls off and is never picked up. The Bhāgavatam ends a regime with one image: a king without a crown, his hair in another man's fist.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.44.38

    ↗
    తం సమ్పరేతం విచకర్ష భూమౌహరిర్యథేభం జగతో విపశ్యత: । హాహేతి శబ్ద: సుమహాంస్తదాభూ-దుదీరిత: సర్వజనైర్నరేన్ద్ర ॥ ౩౮ ॥
